It’s definitely not new. Like, looking back, it was kind of always there. Even in elementary school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets or I’d rush through directions and then do the wrong thing.

her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in lines, waiting for a recruiter to call, or waiting my turn during group volunteering makes me intensely impatient, and I end up checking my phone over and over, Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.

Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Yeah, I think so. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.

It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"When I have to sit through an interview, a meeting, or even a movie, I feel this pressure inside my body like I need to get up, shift around, or do something with my hands or I will crawl out of my skin.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.

Sometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.

It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.

I missed out on some real opportunities because of it, and I’m just kind of scared it’s going to keep happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble remembering to do things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not new. Like, looking back, it was kind of always there. Even in elementary school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets or I’d rush through directions and then do the wrong thing.\n\nAnd my desk was… honestly, it was like a disaster zone. I remember digging around for assignments and finding old papers and random pencil shavings—sorry, that’s gross—but I just couldn’t find what I needed. I also got in trouble for talking out of turn a lot, which I guess isn’t exactly “remembering,” but it was all part of the same school reports, like I wasn’t managing things the way other kids were.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even sitting here, part of me is trying to look normal, but I feel this pressure in my body like I need to move or do something with my hands.\n\nIt happens in interviews or meetings, and even movies, which is annoying because I actually like movies—well, if it’s a theater I start thinking about whether people can tell I’m shifting around too much. It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.

Maybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.

I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.

her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in lines, waiting for a recruiter to call, or waiting my turn during group volunteering makes me intensely impatient, and I end up checking my phone over and over, interrupting, or trying to push things along.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, teachers wrote that I was bright but constantly lost worksheets, rushed through directions, talked out of turn, and had a desk so messy I could not find assignments.\n- key: Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Sleep, maybe I’ll stay up too late scrolling or watching something, but that feels more like bad judgment than being so absorbed I don’t notice I’m tired.

Like last night I was looking at lamps online because my room lighting is weird, and then I ended up reading reviews of blackout curtains, which isn’t even what I needed, and then I was like, why am I doing this—but I still knew I should go to bed. So… I don’t think I have a clear example of forgetting to eat or sleep because I’m absorbed.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I keep forgetting really basic adult stuff. Like paying bills, replying to interview emails, going to appointments I already made, errands I said I’d do… and then I don’t do them. \n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’ll have every intention of handling it, and then suddenly it’s days later. I missed out on some real opportunities because of it, and I’m just kind of scared it’s going to keep happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble remembering to do things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not new. Like, looking back, it was kind of always there. Even in elementary school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets or I’d rush through directions and then do the wrong thing.\n\nAnd my desk was… honestly, it was like a disaster zone. I remember digging around for assignments and finding old papers and random pencil shavings—sorry, that’s gross—but I just couldn’t find what I needed. I also got in trouble for talking out of turn a lot, which I guess isn’t exactly “remembering,” but it was all part of the same school reports, like I wasn’t managing things the way other kids were.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even sitting here, part of me is trying to look normal, but I feel this pressure in my body like I need to move or do something with my hands.\n\nIt happens in interviews or meetings, and even movies, which is annoying because I actually like movies—well, if it’s a theater I start thinking about whether people can tell I’m shifting around too much. It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.\n\nOr sometimes it’s not even something around me, it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, I should check that email,” and then I’m doing that instead. And then twenty minutes later I’m like, wait, I was supposed to be working on the resume. It’s kind of frustrating because I don’t always notice the switch happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetfulness, impulsivity, or restlessness interfered with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply for things, and I’ll have an interview or someone will ask me to follow up, and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I’ll remember at, like, midnight and think, okay I’ll do it tomorrow, and then it’s gone again.\n\nAnd I had this volunteer project I actually really cared about—I liked the people, and it felt like something useful—but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up with the basic back-and-forth and deadlines. Which sounds so small when I say it, like emails and scheduling, but it adds up.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.\n\nMy desk was always a disaster, too. Like, papers just shoved everywhere, and then I couldn’t find the assignment I’d literally just been given. I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? Yeah, I think so. The attention and remembering stuff is honestly what made me come in. Like, I can deal with being fidgety or saying something too fast sometimes, but forgetting basic adult things is really messing things up.

I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.\n\nOr sometimes it’s not even something around me, it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, I should check that email,” and then I’m doing that instead. And then twenty minutes later I’m like, wait, I was supposed to be working on the resume. It’s kind of frustrating because I don’t always notice the switch happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetfulness, impulsivity, or restlessness interfered with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply for things, and I’ll have an interview or someone will ask me to follow up, and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I’ll remember at, like, midnight and think, okay I’ll do it tomorrow, and then it’s gone again.\n\nAnd I had this volunteer project I actually really cared about—I liked the people, and it felt like something useful—but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up with the basic back-and-forth and deadlines. Which sounds so small when I say it, like emails and scheduling, but it adds up.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. Like even when I want to, I keep dropping pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as inattentive or having trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.\n\nMy desk was always a disaster, too. Like, papers just shoved everywhere, and then I couldn’t find the assignment I’d literally just been given. I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll start looking something up and then somehow I’m on a completely different article or video, but then that happens to everyone, right? I did that the other day with, weirdly, kitchen organization videos, even though I don’t really cook much, and then I started thinking I should reorganize my whole cabinet—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI guess I can’t think of one activity where I reliably get so absorbed that hours disappear. It’s more scattered than that, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity where you become so absorbed that you forget to eat or sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Sleep, maybe I’ll stay up too late scrolling or watching something, but that feels more like bad judgment than being so absorbed I don’t notice I’m tired.\n\nLike last night I was looking at lamps online because my room lighting is weird, and then I ended up reading reviews of blackout curtains, which isn’t even what I needed, and then I was like, why am I doing this—but I still knew I should go to bed. So… I don’t think I have a clear example of forgetting to eat or sleep because I’m absorbed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention or remembering things cause you more problems than your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The attention and remembering stuff is honestly what made me come in. Like, I can deal with being fidgety or saying something too fast sometimes, but forgetting basic adult things is really messing things up.\n\nI’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t reply to interview emails in time, or I’ll just completely blank on appointments. And errands too—like I’ll go out for one thing and somehow come back with snacks and shampoo but not the one thing I needed, which sounds funny, but it’s not when it keeps happening. I had this whole folder system for emails at one point, and then I forgot to check the folder, so that kind of defeated the purpose.\n\nBut yeah, the remembering and follow-through problems are costing me actual opportunities now, not just annoying me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe not as significant as the attention stuff, but it’s definitely there. Like if I’m sitting through an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body, like I need to move or get up or do something with my hands.

It’s hard to explain—it’s not just boredom, it’s more like I feel trapped in my own skin for a second. Like I’ll shift around a lot, mess with my sleeves or my phone or whatever’s nearby. I remember in school I used to hate those long lectures where you couldn’t really move, and even now in waiting rooms I’ll be like, okay, how long has it been, should I stand up, would that look weird—sorry, I’m kind of rambling.

So yeah, I guess the restlessness is significant too, just the memory and attention problems feel like they cause bigger fallout.

I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll start looking something up and then somehow I’m on a completely different article or video, but then that happens to everyone, right? I did that the other day with, weirdly, kitchen organization videos, even though I don’t really cook much, and then I started thinking I should reorganize my whole cabinet—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI guess I can’t think of one activity where I reliably get so absorbed that hours disappear. It’s more scattered than that, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity where you become so absorbed that you forget to eat or sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Sleep, maybe I’ll stay up too late scrolling or watching something, but that feels more like bad judgment than being so absorbed I don’t notice I’m tired.\n\nLike last night I was looking at lamps online because my room lighting is weird, and then I ended up reading reviews of blackout curtains, which isn’t even what I needed, and then I was like, why am I doing this—but I still knew I should go to bed. So… I don’t think I have a clear example of forgetting to eat or sleep because I’m absorbed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention or remembering things cause you more problems than your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The attention and remembering stuff is honestly what made me come in. Like, I can deal with being fidgety or saying something too fast sometimes, but forgetting basic adult things is really messing things up.\n\nI’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t reply to interview emails in time, or I’ll just completely blank on appointments. And errands too—like I’ll go out for one thing and somehow come back with snacks and shampoo but not the one thing I needed, which sounds funny, but it’s not when it keeps happening. I had this whole folder system for emails at one point, and then I forgot to check the folder, so that kind of defeated the purpose.\n\nBut yeah, the remembering and follow-through problems are costing me actual opportunities now, not just annoying me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as significant as your problems with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe not as significant as the attention stuff, but it’s definitely there. Like if I’m sitting through an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body, like I need to move or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain—it’s not just boredom, it’s more like I feel trapped in my own skin for a second. Like I’ll shift around a lot, mess with my sleeves or my phone or whatever’s nearby. I remember in school I used to hate those long lectures where you couldn’t really move, and even now in waiting rooms I’ll be like, okay, how long has it been, should I stand up, would that look weird—sorry, I’m kind of rambling.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the restlessness is significant too, just the memory and attention problems feel like they cause bigger fallout.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I remember in school I used to hate those long lectures where you couldn’t really move, and even now in waiting rooms I’ll be like, okay, how long has it been, should I stand up, would that look weird—sorry, I’m kind of rambling.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the restlessness is significant too, just the memory and attention problems feel like they cause bigger fallout.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant problems with social relationships due to your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it significant, honestly. Like, I’m sure I’ve annoyed people by being restless or interrupting sometimes, but I can’t think of a relationship that really fell apart because of that.

I mean, with friends it’s more like… sometimes I worry I’m being too much, but then I also overthink normal texting stuff, like if someone uses a period instead of an exclamation point I’m suddenly like, oh no, are they mad? Which is probably not what you asked. Sorry.

So, no, I don’t think I have a clear example of major social problems from restlessness or impulsivity. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I keep forgetting really basic adult stuff. Like paying bills, replying to interview emails, going to appointments I already made, errands I said I’d do… and then I don’t do them. \n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’ll have every intention of handling it, and then suddenly it’s days later. I missed out on some real opportunities because of it, and I’m just kind of scared it’s going to keep happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble remembering to do things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not new. Like, looking back, it was kind of always there. Even in elementary school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets or I’d rush through directions and then do the wrong thing.\n\nAnd my desk was… honestly, it was like a disaster zone. I also got in trouble for talking out of turn a lot, which I guess isn’t exactly “remembering,” but it was all part of the same school reports, like I wasn’t managing things the way other kids were.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even sitting here, part of me is trying to look normal, but I feel this pressure in my body like I need to move or do something with my hands.\n\nIt happens in interviews or meetings, and even movies, which is annoying because I actually like movies—well, if it’s a theater I start thinking about whether people can tell I’m shifting around too much. It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.\n\nOr sometimes it’s not even something around me, it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, I should check that email,” and then I’m doing that instead. And then twenty minutes later I’m like, wait, I was supposed to be working on the resume. It’s kind of frustrating because I don’t always notice the switch happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetfulness, impulsivity, or restlessness interfered with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply for things, and I’ll have an interview or someone will ask me to follow up, and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I’ll remember at, like, midnight and think, okay I’ll do it tomorrow, and then it’s gone again.\n\nAnd I had this volunteer project I actually really cared about—I liked the people, and it felt like something useful—but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up with the basic back-and-forth and deadlines. Which sounds so small when I say it, like emails and scheduling, but it adds up.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. Like even when I want to, I keep dropping pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as inattentive or having trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.\n\nMy desk was always a disaster, too. Like, papers just shoved everywhere, and then I couldn’t find the assignment I’d literally just been given. I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll start looking something up and then somehow I’m on a completely different article or video, but then that happens to everyone, right? I did that the other day with, weirdly, kitchen organization videos, even though I don’t really cook much, and then I started thinking I should reorganize my whole cabinet—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI guess I can’t think of one activity where I reliably get so absorbed that hours disappear. It’s more scattered than that, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity where you become so absorbed that you forget to eat or sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember being, like, a toddler or anything, but in elementary school it was already a thing.

I remember digging around for assignments and finding old papers and random pencil shavings—sorry, that’s gross—but I just couldn’t find what I needed. I also got in trouble for talking out of turn a lot, which I guess isn’t exactly “remembering,” but it was all part of the same school reports, like I wasn’t managing things the way other kids were.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like even sitting here, part of me is trying to look normal, but I feel this pressure in my body like I need to move or do something with my hands.\n\nIt happens in interviews or meetings, and even movies, which is annoying because I actually like movies—well, if it’s a theater I start thinking about whether people can tell I’m shifting around too much. It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.\n\nOr sometimes it’s not even something around me, it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, I should check that email,” and then I’m doing that instead. And then twenty minutes later I’m like, wait, I was supposed to be working on the resume. It’s kind of frustrating because I don’t always notice the switch happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetfulness, impulsivity, or restlessness interfered with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply for things, and I’ll have an interview or someone will ask me to follow up, and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I’ll remember at, like, midnight and think, okay I’ll do it tomorrow, and then it’s gone again.\n\nAnd I had this volunteer project I actually really cared about—I liked the people, and it felt like something useful—but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up with the basic back-and-forth and deadlines. Which sounds so small when I say it, like emails and scheduling, but it adds up.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. Like even when I want to, I keep dropping pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as inattentive or having trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.\n\nMy desk was always a disaster, too. Like, papers just shoved everywhere, and then I couldn’t find the assignment I’d literally just been given. I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll start looking something up and then somehow I’m on a completely different article or video, but then that happens to everyone, right? I did that the other day with, weirdly, kitchen organization videos, even though I don’t really cook much, and then I started thinking I should reorganize my whole cabinet—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI guess I can’t think of one activity where I reliably get so absorbed that hours disappear. It’s more scattered than that, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity where you become so absorbed that you forget to eat or sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Sleep, maybe I’ll stay up too late scrolling or watching something, but that feels more like bad judgment than being so absorbed I don’t notice I’m tired.\n\nLike last night I was looking at lamps online because my room lighting is weird, and then I ended up reading reviews of blackout curtains, which isn’t even what I needed, and then I was like, why am I doing this—but I still knew I should go to bed. So… I don’t think I have a clear example of forgetting to eat or sleep because I’m absorbed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention or remembering things cause you more problems than your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The attention and remembering stuff is honestly what made me come in. Like, I can deal with being fidgety or saying something too fast sometimes, but forgetting basic adult things is really messing things up.\n\nI’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t reply to interview emails in time, or I’ll just completely blank on appointments. And errands too—like I’ll go out for one thing and somehow come back with snacks and shampoo but not the one thing I needed, which sounds funny, but it’s not when it keeps happening. I had this whole folder system for emails at one point, and then I forgot to check the folder, so that kind of defeated the purpose.\n\nBut yeah, the remembering and follow-through problems are costing me actual opportunities now, not just annoying me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as significant as your problems with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe not as significant as the attention stuff, but it’s definitely there. Like if I’m sitting through an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body, like I need to move or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain—it’s not just boredom, it’s more like I feel trapped in my own skin for a second. Like I’ll shift around a lot, mess with my sleeves or my phone or whatever’s nearby. I remember in school I used to hate those long lectures where you couldn’t really move, and even now in waiting rooms I’ll be like, okay, how long has it been, should I stand up, would that look weird—sorry, I’m kind of rambling.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the restlessness is significant too, just the memory and attention problems feel like they cause bigger fallout.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with social relationships due to your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant, honestly. Like, I’m sure I’ve annoyed people by being restless or interrupting sometimes, but I can’t think of a relationship that really fell apart because of that.\n\nI mean, with friends it’s more like… sometimes I worry I’m being too much, but then I also overthink normal texting stuff, like if someone uses a period instead of an exclamation point I’m suddenly like, oh no, are they mad? Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness for as long as you can remember, even as a young child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Yeah… pretty often. Even not working right now, I still somehow spend the whole day bouncing from thing to thing. It’s like if I have to sit still for too long, I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin unless I change position or get up or fiddle with something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in conversations, I think. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s even finished asking the question because I’m already assuming I know where they’re going with it.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not ideal. I’ll start answering and then realize halfway through that I cut them off or answered a totally different question than what they were actually asking. And then I get embarrassed and try to fix it, but sometimes that just makes me talk more.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for reasons that might seem small to others?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get upset like anyone does, I think, but I’m not sure if it’s out of proportion or anything.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be annoyed and then later think, “Was that too much?” but I don’t really have a clear answer. It also depends what’s going on—sorry, that’s kind of vague. What do you mean by small things, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get extremely upset or angry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say extremely. Like, I do get upset sometimes, obviously, but I don’t know where the line is between normal upset and “too upset.”\n\nSometimes I’ll feel it really strongly in the moment, and then later I’m like, wait, was that a big reaction or was I just tired? Or hungry, honestly—I forget to eat and then everything feels more dramatic, which is probably not what you’re asking. Sorry. I guess I don’t have a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. Like I’ll sit down to update my resume, or do one of those coding practice problems, and then my phone lights up with a text or I hear someone talking outside, and suddenly I’m completely somewhere else.\n\nOr sometimes it’s not even something around me, it’s just a random thought, like, “Oh, I should check that email,” and then I’m doing that instead. And then twenty minutes later I’m like, wait, I was supposed to be working on the resume. It’s kind of frustrating because I don’t always notice the switch happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetfulness, impulsivity, or restlessness interfered with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work-wise especially. Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply for things, and I’ll have an interview or someone will ask me to follow up, and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I’ll remember at, like, midnight and think, okay I’ll do it tomorrow, and then it’s gone again.\n\nAnd I had this volunteer project I actually really cared about—I liked the people, and it felt like something useful—but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up with the basic back-and-forth and deadlines. Which sounds so small when I say it, like emails and scheduling, but it adds up.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. Like even when I want to, I keep dropping pieces of it.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as inattentive or having trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I remember report cards from elementary school saying things like I was bright, but I wasn’t really… organized, I guess. Teachers would write that I lost worksheets all the time or didn’t follow the directions because I rushed through them.\n\nMy desk was always a disaster, too. Like, papers just shoved everywhere, and then I couldn’t find the assignment I’d literally just been given. I also talked out of turn a lot, which—sorry, that’s not exactly paying attention, but it was in the same comments usually. It was always kind of framed like, “She’s capable, but…” which got old pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I’m not usually, like, wanting to go run around or climb things exactly, but if I have to sit still—like in an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie—I get this pressure in my body. Like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there thinking, why is everyone else so calm? And I’m trying to pay attention, but my body feels like it’s buzzing, like if I don’t move I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Even in movie theaters, which is annoying because I like movies, but then I’m changing how I’m sitting or messing with my sleeve or my drink the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, maybe? I’m not sure if I’d call it that. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something and then realize time passed, but I don’t know if I’m tuning out everything around me or just… not paying attention in a normal way.\n\nWhat do you mean exactly—like someone talking to me and I literally don’t hear them, or more like I get absorbed in a task? Because those feel kind of different to me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were so focused on an activity that you didn't notice things going on around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, but nothing really clear is coming up. Like, I get absorbed in things sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if it’s to the point where I’m not noticing what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe if I’m on my phone or reading something, I can miss someone saying my name, but that also feels kind of normal? My roommate does that too when she’s watching TikToks, so I don’t know if that counts. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the wording. I guess I can’t think of a strong example where I was so focused that I totally tuned everything out.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activities where you become so absorbed that you lose track of time for hours?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can lose time doing random things, but “for hours” feels like… I don’t know if I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll start looking something up and then somehow I’m on a completely different article or video, but then that happens to everyone, right? I did that the other day with, weirdly, kitchen organization videos, even though I don’t really cook much, and then I started thinking I should reorganize my whole cabinet—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI guess I can’t think of one activity where I reliably get so absorbed that hours disappear. It’s more scattered than that, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity where you become so absorbed that you forget to eat or sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really? I mean, I’ve definitely delayed eating because I’m doing something, but I don’t think I’ve forgotten in a serious way. Sleep, maybe I’ll stay up too late scrolling or watching something, but that feels more like bad judgment than being so absorbed I don’t notice I’m tired.\n\nLike last night I was looking at lamps online because my room lighting is weird, and then I ended up reading reviews of blackout curtains, which isn’t even what I needed, and then I was like, why am I doing this—but I still knew I should go to bed. So… I don’t think I have a clear example of forgetting to eat or sleep because I’m absorbed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention or remembering things cause you more problems than your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. The attention and remembering stuff is honestly what made me come in. Like, I can deal with being fidgety or saying something too fast sometimes, but forgetting basic adult things is really messing things up.\n\nI’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t reply to interview emails in time, or I’ll just completely blank on appointments. And errands too—like I’ll go out for one thing and somehow come back with snacks and shampoo but not the one thing I needed, which sounds funny, but it’s not when it keeps happening. I had this whole folder system for emails at one point, and then I forgot to check the folder, so that kind of defeated the purpose.\n\nBut yeah, the remembering and follow-through problems are costing me actual opportunities now, not just annoying me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as significant as your problems with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe not as significant as the attention stuff, but it’s definitely there. Like if I’m sitting through an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie, I get this pressure in my body, like I need to move or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain—it’s not just boredom, it’s more like I feel trapped in my own skin for a second. Like I’ll shift around a lot, mess with my sleeves or my phone or whatever’s nearby. I remember in school I used to hate those long lectures where you couldn’t really move, and even now in waiting rooms I’ll be like, okay, how long has it been, should I stand up, would that look weird—sorry, I’m kind of rambling.\n\nSo yeah, I guess the restlessness is significant too, just the memory and attention problems feel like they cause bigger fallout.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with social relationships due to your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it significant, honestly. Like, I’m sure I’ve annoyed people by being restless or interrupting sometimes, but I can’t think of a relationship that really fell apart because of that.\n\nI mean, with friends it’s more like… sometimes I worry I’m being too much, but then I also overthink normal texting stuff, like if someone uses a period instead of an exclamation point I’m suddenly like, oh no, are they mad? Which is probably not what you asked. Sorry.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I have a clear example of major social problems from restlessness or impulsivity. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness for as long as you can remember, even as a young child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember being, like, a toddler or anything, but in elementary school it was already a thing.\n\nTeachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I was always losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them. My desk was just… chaos. Like papers shoved everywhere, and then I’d swear I had the assignment, but it was probably crumpled under old art projects or something.\n\nAnd I’d rush through directions too, like start before I actually understood what we were supposed to do. I also got told I talked out of turn a lot. Which is funny because I remember thinking I was just answering quickly, but apparently it was interrupting. So yeah, looking back, it was definitely there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are 'on the go' or like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty often. Even not working right now, I still somehow spend the whole day bouncing from thing to thing. Like I’ll start laundry, then remember an email, then I’m reorganizing browser tabs for no real reason, then I’m pacing around the kitchen and starting dinner before I’ve finished any of the other stuff.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just sit and be still, even if there’s nothing urgent. I’ll tell myself I’m going to relax, and then five minutes later I’m up doing some random task that I don’t even finish. It feels like I’m always in motion, but not in a productive way, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
